The distance between two points (x, y) and (a, b) is given by the formula √[(x - a)² + (y - b)²]. So, the distance from (x, y) to the focus (2, 3) would be √[(x - 2)² + (y - 3)²].

Now, for the directrix x = -1. The distance from a point (x, y) to this vertical line is calculated using the horizontal distance formula |x - (-1)|, which simplifies to |x + 1|.

Since these two distances must be equal for any point on the parabola, I can set up the equation:

√[(x - 2)² + (y - 3)²] = |x + 1|

To eliminate the square root and absolute value, I'll square both sides of the equation. That should give me a quadratic equation which is easier to handle.

Squaring both sides gives:

(x - 2)² + (y - 3)² = (x + 1)²

Expanding each term step by step:

First, expand (x - 2)²:
(x - 2)(x - 2) = x² - 4x + 4

Then, expand (y - 3)²:
(y - 3)(y - 3) = y² - 6y + 9

Adding these together on the left side:
x² - 4x + 4 + y² - 6y + 9

On the right side, expanding (x + 1)² gives x² + 2x + 1.

So putting it all together:

x² - 4x + 4 + y² - 6y + 9 = x² + 2x + 1

Now, let's subtract x² from both sides to simplify:
-4x + 4 + y² - 6y + 9 = 2x + 1

Combine like terms on the left side:
(-4x) + (y²) + (-6y) + (4 + 9)
Which simplifies to:
y² - 6y - 4x + 13

So now we have:
y² - 6y - 4x + 13 = 2x + 1

Next, bring all terms to one side by subtracting 2x and 1 from both sides:

y² - 6y - 4x + 13 - 2x - 1 = 0
Simplify the like terms:
-4x - 2x is -6x
13 -1 is 12

So, now we have:
y² - 6y - 6x + 12 = 0

To make this equation more standard, I can rearrange it to group y terms together:

y² - 6y = 6x - 12

y² - 6y + 9 = 6x - 12 + 9

This simplifies to:
(y - 3)² = 6x - 3

Wait, actually, perhaps we can express this equation in standard form by rearranging terms:

(y - 3)² = 6(x - 0.5)

Because if I take 6x - 3, that's 6*(x) - 3, so to factor out 6, it becomes 6*(x - (3/6)) which is x - 0.5.

So the equation can be written as:

(y - 3)^2 = 6(x - 0.5)

The given problem involves finding the equation of a parabola with its focus at (0, 3) and directrix y = -3. 

To solve this:
1. **Set up the distance equations**: The point on the parabola (x, y) must be equidistant from the focus (0, 3) and the directrix.
2. **Use the distance formula** for the focus: sqrt(x² + (y - 3)²).
3. **Calculate the distance to the directrix**: |y + 3|.
4. **Set distances equal** and square both sides to eliminate the square root and absolute value, resulting in x² + (y - 3)² = (y + 3)².
5. **Expand and simplify** both sides:
   - Left side: y² - 6y + 9
   - Right side: y² + 6y + 9
6. Subtract common terms to get x² = 12y.

Thus, the equation of the parabola is:

$$x^2 = 12y$$

**Final Answer**
The equation of the parabola is {Final Answer: "x²=12y"}.

The given equation is \( y = (x - 1)^2 + 5 \), which represents a parabola that opens upwards. 

- **Vertex**: The vertex of the parabola is at \( (h, k) \). Comparing with the standard form \( y = a(x - h)^2 + k \), we have \( h = 1 \) and \( k = 5 \). Therefore, the vertex is at \( (1, 5) \).

- **Focus**: The focus of a parabola in this form is located at \( (h, k + \frac{1}{4a}) \), where \( a \) is the coefficient of the squared term. Here, \( a = 1 \). Substituting these values: 
  - Focus x-coordinate remains 1.
  - Focus y-coordinate is \( 5 + \frac{1}{4*1} = 5 + \frac{1}{4} = \frac{20}{4} + \frac{1}{4} = \frac{21}{4} \).
  So, the focus is at \( (1, \frac{21}{4}) \).

- **Axis of Symmetry**: This is a vertical line passing through the vertex. Since the vertex is at \( x = 1 \), the axis of symmetry is \( x = 1 \).

**Final Answer:**

{
  "vertex": [1, 5],
  "focus": [1, 21/4],
  "axis_of_symmetry": "x = 1"
}

To determine the center and radius of the circle defined by the equation \( x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0 \), we'll follow these steps:

### Step 1: Rearrange the Equation
Start by grouping the \( x \) terms and \( y \) terms together:
\[
x^2 + 6x + y^2 - 4y = 12
\]

### Step 2: Complete the Square for \( x \)
To complete the square for the \( x \)-terms, take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( 3 \), and square it to get \( 9 \). Add this inside the equation:
\[
x^2 + 6x + 9 = (x + 3)^2
\]
Now, add this to both sides:
\[
(x + 3)^2 + y^2 - 4y = 12 + 9 = 21
\]

### Step 3: Complete the Square for \( y \)
Similarly, take half of the coefficient of \( y \), which is \( -2 \) (since we need to use the original coefficient before moving terms around), and square it to get \( 4 \). Add this inside the equation:
\[
y^2 - 4y + 4 = (y - 2)^2
\]
Now, add this to both sides:
\[
(x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 21 + 4 = 25
\]

### Step 4: Write in Standard Form
The equation is now in the standard form of a circle:
\[
(x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
\]
From this, we can identify the center and radius.

- **Center** (\( h, k \)): \( (-3, 2) \)
- **Radius**: The square root of the right-hand side:
\[
r = \sqrt{25} = 5
\]

### Final Answer
The circle has a center at \( (-3, 2) \) and a radius of \( 5 \).

   \[

   \]

   \[
   x^2 - 4x + y^2 + 2y = 11

**Solution:**

To find the equation of the circle with center \((-2, 5)\) and passing through the point \((1, 9)\), follow these steps:

1. **Determine the Radius (\(r\)):**
   
   The radius is the distance between the center \((-2, 5)\) and the point \((1, 9)\).

   \[
   r = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2} = \sqrt{(1 - (-2))^2 + (9 - 5)^2} = \sqrt{3^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{9 + 16} = \sqrt{25} = 5
   \]

2. **Write the Standard Equation of the Circle:**
   
   The standard form of a circle's equation with center \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) is:

   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]

   Substituting the known values:

   \[
   (x - (-2))^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 5^2 \\
   \Rightarrow (x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{(x+2)^2+(y-5)^2=25}

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the standard form:** The general equation of a circle centered at (h, k) with radius r is \( (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2 \).

2. **Substitute the center coordinates:** Given the center (1, 2), substitute h=1 and k=2 into the equation:
   \[ (x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = r^2 \]

3. **Determine the radius using the given point:**
   - The circle passes through (4,6). Plug x=4 and y=6 into the equation to find \(r^2\):
     \[ (4 - 1)^2 + (6 - 2)^2 = r^2 \]
     \[ 3^2 + 4^2 = r^2 \]
     \[ 9 + 16 = r^2 \]
     \[ 25 = r^2 \]

4. **Write the complete equation:**
   - Since \(r^2 = 25\), substitute back into the standard form:
     \[ (x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25 \]

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the circle is \((x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25\).

First, the standard form of an ellipse is (x²/a²) + (y²/b²) = 1, where a and b are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes, respectively. But wait, sometimes it can be flipped depending on which denominator is larger. So I should check that.

But wait, I should make sure whether a is under x or y. Since 36 > 9, that means the major axis is along the x-axis because the larger denominator corresponds to the major axis.

Now, eccentricity (e) of an ellipse is given by e = c/a, where c is the distance from the center to each focus. And we know that c² = a² - b². So let me calculate c first.

Given a²=36 and b²=9,
c² = 36 - 9 = 27
So c = √27. Simplifying, √27 is 3√3 because 27 is 9*3, so square root of 9 is 3.

Now, the eccentricity e would be c/a which is (3√3)/6. Let me simplify that: dividing numerator and denominator by 3 gives (√3)/2.

Wait, but let me double-check if I got a and b right because sometimes people get confused about which one is major or minor. Since a² > b², yes, a=6 is the semi-major axis along x-axis, so my calculations should be correct.
</think>

The given ellipse equation is \( \frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \). Here, \(a^2 = 36\) and \(b^2 = 9\), so \(a = 6\) (semi-major axis) and \(b = 3\) (semi-minor axis).

The eccentricity \(e\) is calculated using the formula:
\[ e = \frac{\sqrt{a^2 - b^2}}{a} = \frac{\sqrt{36 - 9}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{27}}{6} = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \]

**Final Answer:**
The eccentricity of the ellipse is \( \boxed{\dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}} \).

For a horizontal ellipse centered at the origin, the standard equation is (x²/a²) + (y²/b²) = 1, where 'a' is the semi-major axis and 'b' is the semi-minor axis. 

Given that the major axis length is 100, the semi-major axis 'a' would be half of that, so a = 50.

Next, I recall the relationship between a, b, and c for an ellipse: a² = b² + c², where c is the distance from the center to each focus. 

From the foci given, since they're at (40, 0) and (-40, 0), the distance 'c' from the center (which should be the midpoint of the foci) to each focus is 40. So c = 40.

Now I can plug in the values into the equation a² = b² + c²:

50² = b² + 40²
2500 = b² + 1600

Subtracting 1600 from both sides gives me:
b² = 900

So, taking the square root of both sides, b = 30.

Now I can write the equation of the ellipse using a² and b²:

x²/2500 + y²/900 = 1

I should check my steps to make sure I didn't make any mistakes. The foci are on the x-axis, so it's a horizontal ellipse. Calculated 'a' correctly as 50 because major axis is 100. The given ellipse has foci at (40, 0) and (-40, 0), indicating it's centered at the origin with a horizontal major axis. The length of the major axis is 100, so the semi-major axis \( a = 50 \). Using the relationship \( c^2 = a^2 - b^2 \) where \( c = 40 \), we find:

\[
b^2 = a^2 - c^2 = 50^2 - 40^2 = 2500 - 1600 = 900
\]

Thus, the standard equation of the ellipse is:
\[
\frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{900} = 1
\]

Final Answer: {x²/2500 + y²/900 = 1}

Okay, so I need to find the equation of an ellipse that passes through two points, (3, 0) and (0, 2), and has its major axis aligned horizontally. Hmm, let's start by recalling what the standard form of an ellipse looks like when it's centered at the origin.

The general equation is (x²/a²) + (y²/b²) = 1, where 'a' is the semi-major axis and 'b' is the semi-minor axis. Since the major axis is horizontal, that means a > b. So, points along the x-axis will have larger distances from the center compared to those on the y-axis.

Now, I know two points that lie on this ellipse: (3, 0) and (0, 2). Let me plug these into the equation to find 'a' and 'b'.

First, plugging in (3, 0):
(3²/a²) + (0²/b²) = 1
That simplifies to (9/a²) = 1. So, solving for a² gives me a² = 9. Therefore, a is 3.

Next, plug in the point (0, 2):
(0²/9) + (2²/b²) = 1
Simplifying that, I get (4/b²) = 1. So, b² must be 4, meaning b is 2.

Putting it all together, the equation of the ellipse should be (x²/9) + (y²/4) = 1. That makes sense because when x is at its maximum or minimum along the major axis, y would be zero, which matches the point (3, 0). Similarly, when y is at its maximum along the minor axis, x would be zero, matching the point (0, 2).

I think that's it! The ellipse equation fits both points and respects the orientation of the major axis.
</think>

The standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin with a horizontal major axis is given by:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

where \( a \) is the semi-major axis and \( b \) is the semi-minor axis.

Given that the ellipse passes through the points (3, 0) and (0, 2):

1. Plugging in (3, 0):
   \[
   \frac{3^2}{a^2} + \frac{0^2}{b^2} = 1 \implies \frac{9}{a^2} = 1 \implies a^2 = 9 \implies a = 3
   \]

2. Plugging in (0, 2):
   \[
   \frac{0^2}{9} + \frac{2^2}{b^2} = 1 \implies \frac{4}{b^2} = 1 \implies b^2 = 4 \implies b = 2
   \]

Therefore, the equation of the ellipse is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{9} + \frac{y^2}{4} = 1
\]

Okay, so I need to find the foci of the ellipse given by the equation x²/4 + y²/9 = 1. Hmm, first, I remember that the standard form of an ellipse is either (x-h)²/a² + (y-k)²/b² = 1 or vice versa, depending on whether it's wider horizontally or vertically.

In this case, comparing to the given equation, x²/4 and y²/9, so since 4 < 9, that means a²=4 and b²=9? Wait, no, actually, I think if the denominator under y² is larger, then the major axis is along the y-axis. So in standard form, it's (x²/b²) + (y²/a²) =1 when a > b.

So here, a² is 9 and b² is 4, which means a=3 and b=2. The center of the ellipse should be at (h,k), but since there are no shifts in the equation, h and k are both zero. So the center is at (0,0).

Now, to find the foci, I remember that c² = a² - b² for an ellipse. Let me calculate c.

So c² = 9 -4=5, which means c=sqrt(5). Since the major axis is along the y-axis, the foci are located at (0, ±c), so (0, sqrt(5)) and (0,-sqrt(5)).

Wait, let me double-check. If a² is under y², then yes, it's vertical, so c would be calculated as sqrt(a² - b²). So that seems right.
</think>

The ellipse has foci at (0, √5) and (0, -√5).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Standard Form:** The given equation is x²/4 + y²/9 = 1, which matches the standard form of an ellipse.

2. **Determine a and b:**
   - Since the denominator under y² (9) is larger than that under x² (4), the major axis is along the y-axis.
   - Thus, a² = 9 → a = 3
   - And b² = 4 → b = 2

3. **Calculate c for Foci:**
   - For an ellipse, c² = a² - b²
   - Substituting values: c² = 9 - 4 = 5
   - Therefore, c = √5

4. **Locate the Foci:**
   - Since the major axis is vertical (along y), foci are at (0, ±c)
   - Thus, foci are at (0, √5) and (0, -√5)

**Final Answer:** {Final Answer: "Foci at (0, ±√5)"}

Okay, so I need to find the equation of an ellipse centered at (-2, 1). The major axis is horizontal and has a length of 12, while the minor axis is vertical with a length of 8. Hmm, let's break this down step by step.

First, I remember that the standard form of an ellipse's equation depends on whether it's oriented horizontally or vertically. Since the major axis is horizontal, the standard form should be:

((x - h)^2)/a^2 + ((y - k)^2)/b^2 = 1

Where (h, k) is the center, a is the semi-major axis, and b is the semi-minor axis.

Given that the center is (-2, 1), I can substitute h with -2 and k with 1. So now the equation becomes:

((x + 2)^2)/a^2 + ((y - 1)^2)/b^2 = 1

Next, I need to find the values of a and b. The major axis length is given as 12, which means the full length from one end to the other is 12. Therefore, the semi-major axis (a) is half of that, so a = 12/2 = 6.

Similarly, the minor axis length is 8, so the semi-minor axis (b) is half of that, which is b = 8/2 = 4.

Now substituting these values into the equation:

((x + 2)^2)/6^2 + ((y - 1)^2)/4^2 = 1

Calculating the squares, we get:

((x + 2)^2)/36 + ((y - 1)^2)/16 = 1

I should double-check to make sure I didn't mix up a and b. Since the major axis is horizontal, a is indeed larger than b, which makes sense because 6 > 4.

The equation of the ellipse centered at (-2, 1) with a horizontal major axis of length 12 and a vertical minor axis of length 8 is derived as follows:

- The standard form for an ellipse with a horizontal major axis is \(\frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1\), where \(a > b\).

- Substituting the center coordinates (-2, 1) gives:
  \[
  \frac{(x + 2)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - 1)^2}{b^2} = 1
  \]

- The length of the major axis is 12, so \(a = 6\). Similarly, the minor axis length is 8, so \(b = 4\).

- Plugging these values into the equation:
  \[
  \frac{(x + 2)^2}{36} + \frac{(y - 1)^2}{16} = 1
  \]

**Answer:**  
The standard form of the ellipse's equation is: \boxed{\dfrac{(x+2)^2}{36}+\dfrac{(y-1)^2}{16}=1}

The given hyperbola equation is \(\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1\). This represents a horizontal hyperbola centered at the origin.

**Asymptotes:**
For a horizontal hyperbola of the form \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\), the asymptotes are given by \(y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x\).

- Here, \(a^2 = 9\) so \(a = 3\).
- And \(b^2 = 16\) so \(b = 4\).

Therefore, the equations of the asymptotes are:
\[ y = \pm \frac{4}{3}x \]

**Foci:**
The distance from the center to each focus (c) is calculated using \(c^2 = a^2 + b^2\).

- Substituting the known values:
  - \(a^2 = 9\)
  - \(b^2 = 16\)

So, \(c^2 = 9 + 16 = 25\) which means \(c = \sqrt{25} = 5\).

Since the hyperbola is horizontal and centered at (0, 0), the foci are located at:
\[ (c, 0) \text{ and } (-c, 0) \]
Which simplifies to:
\[ (5, 0) \text{ and } (-5, 0) \]

**Final Answer:**
- Asymptotes: \(y = \pm \frac{4}{3}x\)
- Foci: \((\pm 5, 0)\)

The given equation \(4x^2 - 9y^2 = 36\) can be converted into the standard form of a hyperbola by dividing each term by 36.

Step-by-step explanation:

1. Start with the original equation:
   \[
   4x^2 - 9y^2 = 36
   \]

2. Divide every term by 36 to normalize the right-hand side to 1:
   \[
   \frac{4x^2}{36} - \frac{9y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36}
   \]

3. Simplify each fraction:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1
   \]

The equation is now in the standard form of a hyperbola.

Okay, so I have this hyperbola equation given as y²/25 - x²/16 = 1. I need to find its center, vertices, foci, and asymptotes. Hmm, let me start by recalling what a hyperbola is and how these terms relate to it.

First, the standard form of a hyperbola centered at (h, k) is either (y - k)²/a² - (x - h)²/b² = 1 for a vertical transverse axis or (x - h)²/a² - (y - k)²/b² = 1 for a horizontal one. In this case, since the y term is positive, it's a vertical hyperbola.

So, comparing the given equation to the standard form, I can see that h and k must be zero because there are no shifts in the terms. That means the center of the hyperbola is at (0, 0). Easy enough.

Next, finding the vertices. For a vertical hyperbola, the vertices are located 'a' units above and below the center along the y-axis. The value under y² is 25, so a² = 25 which means a = 5. Therefore, the vertices are at (0 + 0, 0 ± 5), so (0, 5) and (0, -5).

Now, moving on to the foci. The distance from the center to each focus is 'c', where c² = a² + b². Here, a² is 25 and b² is 16, so c² = 25 + 16 = 41. Thus, c = √41. Since it's a vertical hyperbola, the foci are along the y-axis as well, at (0 ± √41, 0). So the coordinates are (√41, 0) and (-√41, 0).

Next up are the asymptotes. For hyperbolas, the equations of the asymptotes depend on whether it's vertical or horizontal. For a vertical one, they are y = ±(a/b)x + k. Wait, no, actually I think I mixed something up. Let me double-check: for a vertical hyperbola, it should be y = (a/b)(x - h) + k and y = -(a/b)(x - h) + k because the slopes are determined by a over b.

In this case, since the center is at (0, 0), h and k are zero. So substituting in the values, the asymptotes should be y = ±(5/4)x. Because a is 5 and b is 4 from under x² which is 16 so b squared is 16, thus b=4.

Putting it all together: center at (0,0), vertices at (0,5) and (0,-5), foci at (√41, 0) and (-√41, 0), asymptotes y = ±(5/4)x.

Okay, so I need to find the equation of a hyperbola given its foci and vertices. The problem states that the foci are at (0, ±5) and the vertices are at (0, ±3). 

First, I remember that hyperbolas can be either horizontal or vertical. Since both the foci and vertices have the same x-coordinate of 0, this suggests it's a vertical hyperbola. In other words, it opens upwards and downwards.

For a vertical hyperbola centered at (h, k), the standard form is:
\[
\frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
Here, 'a' is the distance from the center to each vertex, and 'c' is the distance from the center to each focus. The relationship between a, b, and c for hyperbolas is:
\[
c^2 = a^2 + b^2
\]

Looking at the given points, both foci are at (0, ±5), so the center of the hyperbola must be at the midpoint of these foci. Since they're symmetric about the origin, the center is (0, 0). Similarly, the vertices are at (0, ±3), so 'a' should be 3 because that's the distance from the center to each vertex.

So plugging in a = 3 and c = 5 into the relationship:
\[
5^2 = 3^2 + b^2
\]
Simplifying this gives:
\[
25 = 9 + b^2
\]
Subtracting 9 from both sides:
\[
b^2 = 16
\]
So b is 4.

Now, substituting a² and b² into the standard equation of the hyperbola:
\[
\frac{y^2}{3^2} - \frac{x^2}{4^2} = 1
\]
Which simplifies to:
\[
\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
\]
That's the equation of the hyperbola.
</think>

To determine the equation of the hyperbola with horizontal transverse axis and asymptotes \( y = \pm 2x \), we start with the standard form for such a hyperbola:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

The slopes of the asymptotes are given by \( \pm \frac{b}{a} \). Since the asymptotes are \( y = \pm 2x \), we have:

\[
\frac{b}{a} = 2 \implies b = 2a
\]

Substituting \( b = 2a \) into the standard form equation, we get:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{(2a)^2} = 1 \implies \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{4a^2} = 1
\]

To simplify, multiply through by \( a^2 \):

\[
x^2 - \frac{y^2}{4} = a^2
\]

Without additional information (like specific points or vertices), the equation remains in terms of \( a \). For simplicity, if we assume \( a = 1 \), then:

\[
x^2 - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1
\]

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the hyperbola is \( x^2 - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1 \).

The equation of the parabola with vertex at (5, -2) opening downward and having a focus at (5, -4) is:

**Final Answer:**  
{Final Answer: "(x - 5)^2 = -8(y + 2)"}

Step-by-Step Explanation:
1. **Identify the vertex form of a vertical parabola**: The standard equation when a parabola opens upward or downward is \[ (x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k) \], where (h, k) is the vertex and p is the distance from the vertex to the focus.
2. **Plug in the vertex coordinates**: Given the vertex is at (5, -2), substitute h=5 and k=-2 into the equation: \[ (x - 5)^2 = 4p(y + 2) \].
3. **Determine p using the focus location**: The focus is at (5, -4). Since it's a vertical parabola opening downward, calculate the distance from the vertex to the focus:
   - The y-coordinate changes from -2 to -4, so Δy = -4 - (-2) = -2. Thus, p = -2.
4. **Substitute p into the equation**: Plug p=-2 into \[ (x - 5)^2 = 4p(y + 2) \] to get: \[ (x - 5)^2 = 4*(-2)(y + 2) \].
5. **Simplify the equation**: Multiply 4 and -2 to obtain \[ (x - 5)^2 = -8(y + 2) \].

Answer: {Final Answer: "(x - 5)^2 = -8(y + 2)"}.

The given quadratic equation \( Y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \) can be converted into vertex form by completing the square.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Start with the given equation:**  
   \( Y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \)

2. **Factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \) from the first two terms:**  
   \( Y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5 \)

3. **Complete the square inside the parentheses:**  
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is 2, resulting in 1.
   - Square this value: \( (1)^2 = 1 \).
   
   So, add and subtract 1 within the equation:
   \( Y = 3(x^2 + 2x + 1 - 1) + 5 \)
   
4. **Rewrite as a perfect square trinomial:**  
   The expression becomes:
   \( Y = 3[(x + 1)^2 - 1] + 5 \)

5. **Distribute the factored coefficient (3) and combine constants:**  
   \( Y = 3(x + 1)^2 - 3 + 5 \)
   
6. **Simplify the constants:**  
   \( Y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2 \)

7. **Final vertex form of the equation is:**
   \( Y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2 \)

**Answer:**  
{Final Answer: "Y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2"}

The given equation \( x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \) can be rewritten in standard form by completing the square. 

1. Rearrange and complete the square:
   \[
   x^2 - 4x + 8y = -12
   \]
   Add 4 to both sides to complete the square for \( x \):
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + 8y = -8
   \]
   
2. Solve for \( y \) and compare with standard form:
   \[
   8y = -(x - 2)^2 - 8 \\
   y = -\frac{(x - 2)^2}{8} - 1
   \]
   Which can be rewritten as:
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 = -8(y + 1)
   \]

The standard form is \( (x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k) \), so the vertex is at \( (h, k) = (2, -1) \).

To solve the problem, we need to determine the set of points \( S \) such that for any point \( P \) in \( S \), the equation \( |PA - PB| = PA + PB \) holds. 

First, we analyze the condition \( |PA - PB| = PA + PB \). This condition can be broken down into two cases:
1. If \( PA \geq PB \), then \( |PA - PB| = PA - PB \). The equation becomes \( PA - PB = PA + PB \), which simplifies to \( -PB = PB \) or \( 2PB = 0 \). This implies \( PB = 0 \), meaning \( P \) must be point \( B \).
2. If \( PB > PA \), then \( |PA - PB| = PB - PA \). The equation becomes \( PB - PA = PA + PB \), which simplifies to \( -PA = PA \) or \( 2PA = 0 \). This implies \( PA = 0 \), meaning \( P \) must be point \( A \).

Thus, the only points that satisfy this condition are \( A \) and \( B \). Therefore, the solution set \( S \) consists of the points \( (0, 0) \) and \( (2, 4) \).

To encapsulate these solutions as equations, we can write them explicitly:
- For point \( A = (0, 0) \): \( x^2 + y^2 = 0 \)
- For point \( B = (2, 4) \): \( (x - 2)^2 + (y - 4)^2 = 0 \)

Combining these, we get the product form:
\[ (x^2 + y^2)((x - 2)^2 + (y - 4)^2) = 0 \]

Thus, the solution set \( S \) is exactly the points where either \( x^2 + y^2 = 0 \) or \( (x - 2)^2 + (y - 4)^2 = 0 \), which are the points \( (0, 0) \) and \( (2, 4) \).

### Final Answer
The solution set consists of the two points \boxed{(0, 0)} and \boxed{(2, 4)}.

Okay, so I have this problem where I need to find the vertex, focus, and axis of symmetry for the parabola given by the equation y = (x - 1)^2 + 5. Hmm, let me think about how to approach this.

First off, I remember that the standard form of a parabola's equation can help identify these features. The general form is either y = a(x - h)^2 + k for a vertical parabola or x = a(y - k)^2 + h for a horizontal one. In this case, since the equation is in terms of y and it's a function of x squared, it must be a vertical parabola.

Looking at the given equation: y = (x - 1)^2 + 5. Comparing this to the standard form y = a(x - h)^2 + k, I can see that h is 1 and k is 5. So, the vertex should be at the point (h, k), which would be (1, 5). That makes sense because in the standard form, (h, k) is the vertex.

Next, I need to find the focus. For a vertical parabola, the formula for the focus is different from that of a horizontal one. The distance from the vertex to the focus is given by p = 1/(4a). Here, 'a' is the coefficient in front of the squared term. In this equation, a is 1 because it's just (x - 1)^2 without any other coefficients.

Calculating p: p = 1/(4*1) = 1/4. Since the parabola opens upwards (because a is positive), the focus will be above the vertex. So, adding p to the y-coordinate of the vertex gives the focus's location. Therefore, the focus is at (1, 5 + 1/4) which simplifies to (1, 21/4).

Now, for the axis of symmetry. In a vertical parabola, this is straightforward because it's just the vertical line that passes through the vertex. Since the vertex is at x = 1, the axis of symmetry must be the line x = 1.

Putting it all together: the vertex is (1,5), the focus is (1,21/4), and the axis of symmetry is x=1.
</think>

The given equation \( y = (x - 1)^2 + 5 \) represents a vertical parabola. 

- **Vertex**: The vertex form of a parabola is \( y = a(x - h)^2 + k \). Here, comparing with the standard form, we get the vertex at \( (h, k) = (1, 5) \).
  
- **Focus**: For a vertical parabola, the focus is located at \( (h, k + \frac{1}{4a}) \). With \( a = 1 \), this becomes \( (1, 5 + \frac{1}{4}) = (1, \frac{21}{4}) \).

- **Axis of Symmetry**: This is the vertical line passing through the vertex, so it's \( x = 1 \).

The given equation represents a circle in general form. To find its center and radius, we can rewrite it in the standard form by completing the square for both x and y terms.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0\]

2. **Rearrange the terms to group like variables:**
   \[(x^2 + 6x) + (y^2 - 4y) = 12\]

3. **Complete the square for the x terms:**
   - Take the coefficient of \(x\) which is 6, divide by 2 to get 3.
   - Square it to get \(3^2 = 9\).
   - Add and subtract this value inside the equation.

4. **Similarly complete the square for the y terms:**
   - Take the coefficient of \(y\) which is -4, divide by 2 to get -2.
   - Square it to get \((-2)^2 = 4\).
   - Add and subtract this value inside the equation.

5. **Adjust the constants accordingly on both sides:**
   \[(x^2 + 6x + 9) + (y^2 - 4y + 4) = 12 + 9 + 4\]
   Simplifying:
   \[(x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25\]

6. **Compare with the standard form of a circle’s equation:**
   \[(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2\]
   Here, \(h = -3\), \(k = 2\), and \(r^2 = 25\).

7. **Extract the center and radius:**
   - Center of the circle is \((-3, 2)\).
   - Radius of the circle is \(5\) (since \(r = \sqrt{25}\)).

**Answer:**
The center of the circle is (-3, 2) and its radius is 5.

The given equation \( x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0 \) can be rewritten in the standard form of a circle's equation by completing the square.

Step-by-step explanation:

1. **Group like terms:**
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11
   \]

2. **Complete the square for \( x \):**
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( -4/2 = -2 \).
   - Square it: \( (-2)^2 = 4 \).
   - Add and subtract 4 inside the equation.

3. **Complete the square for \( y \):**
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( y \), which is \( 2/2 = 1 \).
   - Square it: \( (1)^2 = 1 \).
   - Add and subtract 1 inside the equation.

4. **Adjust the equation to maintain balance:**
   Since we added 4 and 1, add them to the right side:
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x + 4) + (y^2 + 2y + 1) = 11 + 4 + 1
   \]

5. **Rewrite as perfect squares:**
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
   \]

The final standard form of the circle is:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 4^2
\]
So, the equation in standard form is \( (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16 \).

Final Answer: {Standard Form Equation: "(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16"}

(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **General Equation of a Circle:**  
   The standard equation for a circle with center \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) is:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   
2. **Substitute the Center Coordinates:**  
   Given the center at (1, 2), we substitute \(h = 1\) and \(k = 2\):
   \[
   (x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = r^2
   \]
   
3. **Determine the Radius Using the Point:**  
   The circle passes through the point (4, 6). Substitute \(x = 4\) and \(y = 6\) into the equation:
   \[
   (4 - 1)^2 + (6 - 2)^2 = r^2
   \]
   
4. **Calculate Radius Squared:**

   \[
   3^2 + 4^2 = r^2 \\
   9 + 16 = r^2 \\
   r^2 = 25
   \]
   
5. **Complete the Equation:**  
   Substitute \(r^2 = 25\) back into the equation:
   \[
   (x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the circle is \((x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25\).

\frac{x^2}{b^2} + \frac{y^2}{a^2} = 1
\]

Given that the endpoints of the major axis are (0,7) and (0,-7), we find \(a = 7\). The foci are at (0,5) and (0,-5), so \(c = 5\).

Using the relationship \(c^2 = a^2 - b^2\):

\[
b^2 = a^2 - c^2 = 49 - 25 = 24
\]

Thus, the equation of the ellipse is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{24} + \frac{y^2}{49} = 1
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify Standard Form:** Recognize that the equation is in standard form for an ellipse: \( \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 \), where \( b > a \) indicating a vertical major axis.

2. **Determine Semi-Axis Lengths:** From the denominators, identify \( a^2 = 4 \) so \( a = 2 \) and \( b^2 = 9 \) so \( b = 3 \).

3. **Find Vertices:** The vertices along the major axis (y-axis) are at \( (0, \pm b) = (0, \pm 3) \). Co-vertices along the minor axis (x-axis) are at \( (\pm a, 0) = (\pm 2, 0) \).

4. **Calculate Foci:** The distance from the center to foci is \( c = \sqrt{b^2 - a^2} = \sqrt{9 - 4} = \sqrt{5} \). Thus, foci are at \( (0, \pm \sqrt{5}) \).

For vertical transverse axis (opening up and down):
(y - k)²/a² - (x - h)²/b² = 1

And for horizontal transverse axis:
(x - h)²/a² - (y - k)²/b² = 1

Looking at the given equation, y²/25 - x²/16 = 1, I see that it's in the form of (y²)/a² - (x²)/b² = 1. So this must be a vertical transverse axis hyperbola.

Now, comparing with the standard form:
(y - k)²/a² - (x - h)²/b² = 1

I can see that both terms are subtracted and have positive denominators, which is correct for a vertical hyperbola. The center of the hyperbola is at the point (h, k). In this equation, there's no shift because there are just y² and x² terms without any added constants inside the squares.

So that means h = 0 and k = 0. Therefore, the center is at (0, 0).

Given the parametric equations:

1. \( \frac{x}{a} = \sinh(\theta) \)
2. \( \frac{y}{b} = \cosh(\theta) - k \)

We need to find the relationship between \( x \) and \( y \).

First, we express \( \sinh(\theta) \) and \( \cosh(\theta) \) in terms of \( x \) and \( y \):

- From equation 1: \( \sinh(\theta) = \frac{x}{a} \)
- From equation 2: \( \cosh(\theta) = \frac{y + k}{b} \)

We use the identity for hyperbolic functions:

\[ \cosh^2(\theta) - \sinh^2(\theta) = 1 \]

Substituting the expressions from equations 1 and 2:

\[ \left( \frac{y + k}{b} \right)^2 - \left( \frac{x}{a} \right)^2 = 1 \]

Simplifying this, we get:

\[ \frac{(y + k)^2}{b^2} - \frac{x^2}{a^2} = 1 \]

Thus, the relationship between \( x \) and \( y \) is given by the hyperbola equation:

\[
\boxed{\frac{(y + k)^2}{b^2} - \frac{x^2}{a^2} = 1}
\]

Now, I know that for hyperbolas, the distance from the center to the vertices is 'a' and the distance from the center to the foci is 'c'. The relationship between a, b, and c is given by c² = a² + b². That's an important formula here.

Looking at the points given, the vertices are at (0, ±3), so that means a = 3 because it's the distance from the center (which is at the origin since there's no shift mentioned) to the vertex. Similarly, the foci are at (0, ±5), so c = 5.

The hyperbola has foci at (0, ±5) and vertices at (0, ±3). Since both are on the y-axis, it's a vertical hyperbola with standard form:

\[
\frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Here, \( a = 3 \) and \( c = 5 \). Using \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \):

\[
25 = 9 + b^2 \implies b^2 = 16
\]

So the equation is:

\[
\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
\]

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the hyperbola is \(\boxed{\dfrac{y^2}{9} - \dfrac{x^2}{16} = 1}\).

Now, I need to figure out the value of p. In a parabola, p represents the distance from the vertex to the focus (and also from the vertex to the directrix). The sign of p tells us the direction in which the parabola opens: if p is positive, it opens upward; if p is negative, it opens downward.

Given that the focus is at (0, 4), and the vertex is at (0,0), the distance between them is 4 units. So p must be 4 because moving up from the vertex by 4 units gives us the focus at (0,4). Since p is positive, the parabola opens upward.

The given quadratic equation \( x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \) can be converted into its standard form by completing the square and performing a translation.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Start with the original equation:**
   \[
   x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0
   \]

2. **Rearrange the terms to group the \( x \) terms together:**
   \[
   x^2 - 4x = -8y - 12
   \]

3. **Complete the square for the quadratic expression in \( x \):**
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( -\frac{4}{2} = -2 \).
   - Square this value: \( (-2)^2 = 4 \).

4. **Add and subtract this square inside the equation to complete the square:**
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x + 4) - 4 = -8y - 12
   \]
   
5. **Simplify the left side by writing it as a perfect square:**
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 - 4 = -8y - 12
   \]

6. **Move constants to the right side of the equation:**
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 = -8y - 12 + 4
   \]
   
7. **Simplify the right side by combining like terms:**
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 = -8y - 8
   \]

8. **Factor out the common factor on the right side:**
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 = -8(y + 1)
   \]
   
9. **Let \( X = x - 2 \) and \( Y = y + 1 \) to translate the vertex to the origin:
    \[
    X^2 = -8Y
    \]

This is now in standard form, where the vertex of the parabola is at the origin (0,0).

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{X^2 = -8Y}
\]

The given focus and directrix lead to a degenerate case where the set of points equidistant from both is a vertical line \(x = 0\), not a parabola.

Step-by-step explanation:

1. **Given:**
   - Focus at \((0, 3)\)
   - Directrix: \(y = 3\)

2. **Equation Setup for Parabola:**
   For any point \((x, y)\) on the parabola,
   \[
   \text{Distance to focus} = \sqrt{(x - 0)^2 + (y - 3)^2}
   \]
   and
   \[
   \text{Distance to directrix} = |y - 3|
   \]

3. **Setting Distances Equal:**
   \[
   \sqrt{x^2 + (y - 3)^2} = |y - 3|
   \]

4. **Squaring Both Sides:**
   \[
   x^2 + (y - 3)^2 = (y - 3)^2
   \]
   Simplifying:
   \[
   x^2 = 0 \implies x = 0
   \]

5. **Conclusion:**
   The equation \(x = 0\) represents a vertical line, which is not a parabola but a degenerate conic section.

The given equation of the circle is:

\[ x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0 \]

To find the center and radius, we'll rewrite this in the standard form by completing the square.

\[
(x^2 + 6x) + (y^2 - 4y) = 12
\]

\[
(x^2 + 6x + 9) + (y^2 - 4y) = 12 + 9

\[

\]
\[

The given equation \( x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0 \) can be converted into the standard form of a circle by completing the square.

Step-by-step explanation:
1. Group like terms: \( (x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11 \).
2. Complete the square for x: add and subtract \( (4/2)^2 = 4 \).
3. Complete the square for y: add and subtract \( (2/2)^2 = 1 \).
4. The equation becomes: \( (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16 \).

Final Answer:
The circle has center at (2, -1) and radius 4.

Plugging in the values:
r = √[(1 - (-2))^2 + (9 - 5)^2] = √[3^2 + 4^2] = √(9 + 16) = √25 = 5

Now, I'll write the standard form of the circle's equation using the formula:
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2

(x - (-2))^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 5^2

**Solution:**

To find the equation of a circle with center at \((-2, 5)\) and passing through the point \((1, 9)\), we can use the standard form of the circle's equation:

\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]

where:
- \((h, k)\) is the center of the circle,
- \(r\) is the radius.

**Step 1: Identify the Center**

From the given information:
\[
(h, k) = (-2, 5)
\]

**Step 2: Calculate the Radius**

The radius \(r\) is the distance between the center \((-2, 5)\) and the point \((1, 9)\). We use the distance formula:

\[
r = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]

Plugging in the values:
\[
r = \sqrt{(1 - (-2))^2 + (9 - 5)^2} \\
= \sqrt{(3)^2 + (4)^2} \\
= \sqrt{9 + 16} \\
= \sqrt{25} \\
= 5
\]

**Step 3: Write the Equation of the Circle**

Substituting \(h = -2\), \(k = 5\), and \(r = 5\) into the standard form:

\[
(x - (-2))^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 5^2 \\
(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25
\]

**Final Answer:**

The equation of the circle is:
\[
\boxed{(x+2)^{2}+(y-5)^{2}=25}
\]

**Solution:**

We are given:
- The line \( Y = x + 3 \)
- The circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \)

To find the intersection points, substitute \( y \) from the line equation into the circle's equation:

\[
x^2 + (x + 3)^2 = 17
\]

Expand and simplify:
\[
x^2 + x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17 \\
2x^2 + 6x - 8 = 0 \\
x^2 + 3x - 4 = 0
\]

Solve the quadratic equation using the quadratic formula \( x = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a} \):
- Here, \( a = 1 \), \( b = 3 \), and \( c = -4 \)
- Discriminant: \( D = 9 + 16 = 25 \)

\[
x = \frac{-3 \pm 5}{2}
\]

So,
- \( x_1 = \frac{2}{2} = 1 \)
- \( x_2 = \frac{-8}{2} = -4 \)

Find corresponding \( y \) values:
- For \( x = 1 \): \( y = 1 + 3 = 4 \)
- For \( x = -4 \): \( y = -4 + 3 = -1 \)

**Final Answer:**

The intersection points are \( (1, 4) \) and \( (-4, -1) \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **General Equation:** The standard form of a circle's equation is \((x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2\), where (h, k) is the center and r is the radius.

2. **Substitute Center Coordinates:** Given center (1, 2), substitute into the equation:  
   \[
   (x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = r^2
   \]

3. **Find Radius Using Point on Circle:** The circle passes through (4, 6). Substitute x=4 and y=6 to find \(r^2\):  
   \[

   3^2 + 4^2 = r^2 \\
   9 + 16 = r^2 \\
   25 = r^2
   \]

4. **Determine Radius:**  
   \(r = \sqrt{25} = 5\)

5. **Complete the Equation:** Substitute \(r^2 = 25\) back into the equation:  
   \[
   (x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
   \]

**Final Answer:**

Okay, so I need to find the equation of an ellipse centered at (0, 0) with a major axis length of 10 and a minor axis length of 6. Let me think step by step about how to approach this.

First, I remember that the standard form of an ellipse's equation when it's centered at the origin depends on whether the major axis is horizontal or vertical. Since the problem mentions it's an "horizontal" major axis, that means the major axis is along the x-axis.

The general formula for an ellipse with a horizontal major axis is:
(x²/a²) + (y²/b²) = 1

Here, 'a' represents the semi-major axis length, and 'b' is the semi-minor axis length. 

Now, the problem states that the major axis has a length of 10. Since the major axis is twice the length of the semi-major axis (2a), I can find 'a' by dividing 10 by 2. So, 10 divided by 2 gives me a = 5.

Similarly, the minor axis is given as 6 in length. Using the same logic, since the minor axis is twice the semi-minor axis (2b), b would be 6 divided by 2, which is 3.

So now I have a = 5 and b = 3. Plugging these into the standard equation:

(x²/5²) + (y²/3²) = 1

Which simplifies to:
x²/25 + y²/9 = 1

The equation of the ellipse centered at (0, 0) with a horizontal major axis of length 10 and a minor axis of length 6 is:

$$\frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1$$

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the type of ellipse:** Since the major axis is horizontal, we use the standard form of the equation for a horizontally oriented ellipse.

   $$\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$$

2. **Determine the semi-major axis (a):** The length of the major axis is 10, so half of that (the semi-major axis) is:

   $$a = \frac{10}{2} = 5$$

3. **Determine the semi-minor axis (b):** The length of the minor axis is 6, so half of that (the semi-minor axis) is:

   $$b = \frac{6}{2} = 3$$

4. **Plug a and b into the standard equation:**

   Substitute \(a^2 = 5^2 = 25\) and \(b^2 = 3^2 = 9\) into the equation.

   $$\frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1$$

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the ellipse is $\boxed{\frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1}$.

The given equation 9x² + 4y² = 36 is converted into the standard form of an ellipse by dividing each term by 36, resulting in (x²)/4 + (y²)/9 = 1.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[
   9x^2 + 4y^2 = 36
   \]

2. **Divide each term by 36 to normalize the equation and set it equal to 1:**
   \[
   \frac{9x^2}{36} + \frac{4y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36}
   \]
   
3. **Simplify each fraction:**
   - For the x-term: \( \frac{9x^2}{36} = \frac{x^2}{4} \)
   - For the y-term: \( \frac{4y^2}{36} = \frac{y^2}{9} \)
   
   So, the equation becomes:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
   \]

4. **Final Standard Form of the Ellipse:**
   The equation is now in the standard form \( \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 \), where:
   - \( a^2 = 4 \) (semi-minor axis)
   - \( b^2 = 9 \) (semi-major axis)

**Answer:**  
The standard form of the given equation is \( \frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \).

\]

\[
\frac{(x + 2)^2}{36} + \frac{(y - 1)^2}{16} = 1
\]

This equation reflects the given lengths for the major and minor axes, with 'a' being half of 12 (6) and 'b' being half of 8 (4).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Center:** The center is at (-2, 1), so h = -2 and k = 1.

2. **Determine Semi-Axis Lengths:**
   - Major axis length (2a) = 12 ⇒ a = 6.
   - Minor axis length (2b) = 8 ⇒ b = 4.

   \[
   \frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]

4. **Substitute Values:**
   
   \[
   \frac{(x + 2)^2}{6^2} + \frac{(y - 1)^2}{4^2} = 1 ⇒ \frac{(x + 2)^2}{36} + \frac{(y - 1)^2}{16} = 1
   \]

For such a hyperbola:
- The vertices are located at (±a, 0).
- The foci are located at (±c, 0), where c² = a² + b².
- The asymptotes are the lines y = ±(b/a)x.

Looking back at the given equation x²/4 - y²/9 = 1, I can see that a² is 4 and b² is 9. So, taking square roots:
a = √4 = 2
b = √9 = 3

The vertices are at (±a, 0), which would be (±2, 0).

For the asymptotes, using the formula y = ±(b/a)x, substituting a=2 and b=3 gives y = ±(3/2)x.

- Foci: (±√13, 0)
- Vertices: (±2, 0)
- Asymptotes: y = ±(3/2)x

The hyperbola \( \frac{x^2}{4} - \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \) is a standard horizontal hyperbola with:

- **Vertices**: At points where \( x = \pm a \), so at (±2, 0).
- **Foci**: Calculated using \( c = \sqrt{a^2 + b^2} = \sqrt{4 + 9} = \sqrt{13} \), located at (±√13, 0).
- **Asymptotes**: Given by the lines \( y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x = \pm \frac{3}{2}x \).

1134 ÷7=162; 42÷7=6

So y=(162 +6√629)/16, which simplifies to (81 +3√629)/8.

Which is:

(162 -6√629)/16, simplifies to (81 -3√629)/8.

Thus solutions are:

x=(21 ±√629)/4 and y=(81 ±3√629)/8

Given the equations:

\[ \sqrt{x^2 - 6x} = \sqrt{3y + 5} \]
\[ y = \frac{x^2 + 4}{7} \]

We start by squaring both sides of the first equation:

\[ x^2 - 6x = 3y + 5 \]

Substitute \( y = \frac{x^2 + 4}{7} \) into the equation:

\[ x^2 - 6x = 3\left(\frac{x^2 + 4}{7}\right) + 5 \]

Multiply both sides by 7 to clear the fraction:

\[ 7(x^2 - 6x) = 3(x^2 + 4) + 35 \]

Expand and simplify:

\[ 7x^2 - 42x = 3x^2 + 12 + 35 \]
\[ 7x^2 - 42x = 3x^2 + 47 \]
\[ 4x^2 - 42x - 47 = 0 \]

Solve this quadratic equation using the discriminant:

\[ x = \frac{42 \pm \sqrt{(-42)^2 - 4 \cdot 4 \cdot (-47)}}{2 \cdot 4} \]
\[ x = \frac{42 \pm \sqrt{1764 + 752}}{8} \]
\[ x = \frac{42 \pm \sqrt{2516}}{8} \]
\[ x = \frac{42 \pm 2\sqrt{629}}{8} \]
\[ x = \frac{21 \pm \sqrt{629}}{4} \]

Next, substitute \( x \) back into the expression for \( y \):

For \( x = \frac{21 + \sqrt{629}}{4} \):
\[ y = \frac{\left(\frac{21 + \sqrt{629}}{4}\right)^2 + 4}{7} \]
\[ y = \frac{\frac{(21 + \sqrt{629})^2}{16} + 4}{7} \]
\[ y = \frac{\frac{1070 + 42\sqrt{629}}{16} + \frac{64}{16}}{7} \]
\[ y = \frac{\frac{1134 + 42\sqrt{629}}{16}}{7} \]
\[ y = \frac{81 + 3\sqrt{629}}{8} \]

For \( x = \frac{21 - \sqrt{629}}{4} \):
\[ y = \frac{\left(\frac{21 - \sqrt{629}}{4}\right)^2 + 4}{7} \]
\[ y = \frac{\frac{(21 - \sqrt{629})^2}{16} + 4}{7} \]
\[ y = \frac{\frac{1070 - 42\sqrt{629}}{16} + \frac{64}{16}}{7} \]
\[ y = \frac{\frac{1134 - 42\sqrt{629}}{16}}{7} \]
\[ y = \frac{81 - 3\sqrt{629}}{8} \]

Thus, the solutions are:

\[ x = \boxed{\dfrac{21 + \sqrt{629}}{4}} \text{ and } y = \boxed{\dfrac{81 + 3\sqrt{629}}{8}} \]
or
\[ x = \boxed{\dfrac{21 - \sqrt{629}}{4}} \text{ and } y = \boxed{\dfrac{81 - 3\sqrt{629}}{8}} \]

Okay, so I have to find the equation of a hyperbola given its foci and vertices. The problem says that the foci are at (0, ±5) and the vertices are at (0, ±3). Let me recall what I know about hyperbolas.

First, I remember that hyperbolas can be oriented either vertically or horizontally. Since both the foci and vertices are on the y-axis (they have 0 for their x-coordinates), this must be a vertical hyperbola. The standard form of a vertical hyperbola is:

(y²/a²) - (x²/b²) = 1

Where 'a' is the distance from the center to each vertex, and 'c' is the distance from the center to each focus. I also remember that for hyperbolas, the relationship between a, b, and c is given by:

c² = a² + b²

So, in this case, since the vertices are at (0, ±3), that means a = 3 because 'a' is the distance from the center to the vertex. Similarly, the foci are at (0, ±5), so c = 5.

Plugging these into the relationship:

c² = a² + b²  
(5)² = (3)² + b²  
25 = 9 + b²  
b² = 25 - 9  
b² = 16

So, now that I have a² and b², I can plug them back into the standard form equation.

a² is 9, so:

(y²/9) - (x²/16) = 1

Wait, let me double-check to make sure I didn't mix up any numbers. Foci at ±5 means c=5, vertices at ±3 means a=3. Then b²=c²-a²=25-9=16. Yes, that seems correct.

So the equation is (y²/9) - (x²/16) = 1.
</think>

The hyperbola has its foci and vertices along the y-axis, indicating a vertical orientation. Using the given points:

- Vertices at (0, ±3): \( a = 3 \)
- Foci at (0, ±5): \( c = 5 \)

Using the relationship \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \):

\( 25 = 9 + b^2 \)  
\( b^2 = 16 \)

The standard form of the hyperbola is:

\[
\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
\]

Final Answer: {Final Answer: "(\dfrac{y^2}{9}) - (\dfrac{x^2}{16}) = 1"}
